(a) Real-world RL incurs costly physical interaction. (b) Forward-rollout world-model RL spends much of its interaction budget on less informative exploration. (c) Our proposed Rollback enables high-value exploration and dense supervision through experiencebacktracking tree search, while real-world feedback refines the world model.Our key insight is therefore to redesign world-model-based policy learning around training-time experience backtracking primitive, repeatedly rewinding to and reconsidering decisions for informative data generation. The world model becomes not merely surrogate simulator, but rewindable training environment. action continuations from the same history. This enables the policy to reconsider what it could have done differently at particular decision rather than restarting exploration from scratch. Fully exploiting this capability, however, requires addressing three key challenges as shown in Figure 1. (i) Limited informative exploration. Not all decisions are equally worth revisiting: along trajectory, few critical decisions determine the outcome, while alternatives to most others lead to similar results. Independent rollouts cannot identify these consequential decisions or adaptively allocate exploration among them (Browne et al., 2012; Ecoffet et al., 2020). (ii) Sparse decision-level supervision. Trajectory-level feedback does not reveal which intermediate actions determine the outcome (Andrychowicz et al., 2017; Tan et al., 2025). Treating continuations independently preserves this credit-assignment problem, whereas learning an additional value model introduces extra modeling assumptions and potential estimation bias (Fujimoto et al., 2018; Lu et al., 2025; Schulman et al., 2015). (iii) Biased world-model predictions. Branching from policy-selected contexts may shift interactions beyond the world models training distribution, causing prediction errors to accumulate or be exploited during optimization (Chen et al., 2026; Janner et al., 2019; Yu et al., 2020, 2021). Without real-world grounding, imagined gains may not transfer to real robots. To operationalize training-time experience backtracking and address these challenges, we present Rollback, policy improvement paradigm that turns world models from passive simulators into counterfactual engines. Rollback comprises three key components: (i) Experience-backtracking tree search. Starting from successful and failed real-world trajectories, Rollback rolls back to intermediate decision points and branches into alternative action chunks. Monte Carlo tree search adaptively allocates simulation budget toward decisions whose alternatives lead to divergent outcomes, avoiding exhaustive branching (Browne et al., 2012; Hubert et al., 2021; Silver et al., 2016). (ii) Tree-structured value estimation. Since sibling branches share decision context, contrasting their downstream outcomes isolates the effect of individual actions. Subtree-level outcome aggregation yields fine-grained, actionlevel advantage supervision without an additional learned value model. (iii) World modelpolicy co-evolution. scalable training system unifies distributed world-model search with asynchronous real-robot execution. Real-world experience continually refines the world model, which in turn provides more faithful imagination for search and policy improvement. We call this test-time physics grounding: after pretraining, while the world model serves as the training environment for policy, real transitions collected by that policy correct its predicted dynamics in the states 2 the policy actually visits. Most existing action-conditioned world models are trained offline and then used as fixed simulators (Guo et al., 2025a; Jiang et al., 2025; Yang et al., 2023; Zhu et al., 2024), which suffices for evaluation but not for policy learning, because an improving policy moves into exactly the states the simulator has seen least. We evaluate Rollback on three challenging real-world robotics tasks: block extraction,"

Jiafu Tang1,2,,, Yifan Xu1,2,, Eric Tang3, Junjie Zhao4, Shipeng Luo5 Te Sun4, Haoxuan Hu1, Yiyan Ji1, Xinlong Chen5, Zihao Wang2 Yang Shi6,2,, Yuanxing Zhang2,, Pengfei Wan2, Jiaheng Liu1, 1NJU 2Kling AI 3TJU 4SJTU 5CASIA 6PKU Correspondence: liujiaheng@nju.edu.cn Project page: https://codecap-bench.github.io/ Abstract Video captioning aims to preserve visual information through compact descriptions, predominantly using natural language. However, precisely expressing fine-grained spatial structures and temporal dynamics in natural language often leads to lengthy and potentially ambiguous descriptions. In this work, we explore Code as Video Caption, paradigm where executable programs serve as complementary representation of visual content through graphical primitives, layout rules, and functions of time. To examine whether code captions effectively preserve video information, we compare them with natural language captions on downstream question answering and video reconstruction tasks. Our results show that code captions achieve superior reasoning accuracy and reconstruction fidelity. Despite these advantages, realizing the potential of code captioning depends on whether current multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can generate faithful code captions. To systematically assess this capability, we introduce CodeCap-Bench, which comprises 323 videos spanning five categories of structured 2D content, along with fine-grained annotations and multilevel evaluation framework. Extensive experiments reveal that while leading proprietary MLLMs demonstrate promising code captioning capabilities, they still struggle to faithfully capture complex temporal dynamics, with open-source models lagging substantially behind. Through large-scale evaluation across multiple benchmarks and model backbones, we find many dedicated memory systems underperform naive RAG in many cases, indicating negative net value. Taking RAG as reference, we dissect the net value of memory into the gains and losses over direct retrieval, termed memory benefit and memory tax. These gains and losses arise because memory process changes how the raw information is organized and accessed. We view such changes as the double-edged deviations from the raw history. They are originally designed and introduced to benefit the task, for example by reorganizing facts or distilling preferences, but some may also remove or obscure evidence, giving rise to memory tax. Since memory benefit reflects the expected gains of its design, we then focus more on the overlooked memory tax, study how it arises from the system and evaluation side, and generalize the concept to more application scenarios of memory. We hope the notion of memory tax can provide new lens and shift more attention from standalone performance to the net value of memory, informing more reliable memory designs and evaluations. We will open-source the code and results to the community. Text:"The Trinity: Capability-Driven Recursive Self-Improvement for Multimodal Agents 2026-10- (cid:128) Project Page GitHub Abstract Multimodal intelligence can be viewed as human-guided co-evolution between worlds and agents through interaction. However, scaling this process faces two bottlenecks: constructing heterogeneous, verifiable environments is costly, and manual Harness iteration cannot keep pace with growing task diversity and complexity. key insight is that tasks with different modalities and surface forms can share the same underlying capability requirements. We therefore introduce The Trinity, recursive self-improvement framework coupling world generation with Harness evolution through three roles: Creator, Explorer, and Evolver. Drawing on verified execution trajectories, Evolver shifts the basis of task organization from surface categories to shared requirements, reorganizing the original task tree into hierarchical capability tree. We introduce Capability-Driven Real2Sim2Real: for heterogeneous tasks at the same node, Creator abstracts their common requirements into simplified, verifiable simulated worlds for capability learning, while evaluation on original tasks measures transfer. Along this capability tree, Explorer progressively evolves reusable capability modules through selective inheritance, forming Harness tree whose validated updates accumulate across iterations within fixed topology. The resulting Harnesses specialize in different capabilities, motivating Capability-Aware Harness Router that uses public task information to select compatible module configurations from the tree. Our evolved Harnesses improve mean performance by 34 points across 28 training benchmarks and 26 points across 18 unseen benchmarks, including two real-world embodied tasks. Text:"What Effort Level Should Choose? On the Economics of Reasoning Effort in LLM Agents Kaiser Sun1, Yongchan Kwon2, Zain Hasan2, Shang Zhu2, Kaitlyn Zhou3,2, Federico Bianchi2 1Johns Hopkins University 2Together AI 3Cornell University Corresponding authors T T Generative models increasingly expose reasoning effort setting that lets users scale inference-time compute, trading higher cost for potentially improved performance. Users must choose an effort level before knowing whether extra reasoning will help, and even successful run does not reveal whether the effort purchased was necessary. Reasoning effort is therefore credence good: buyers observe the outcome but cannot assess the marginal value of the computation they paid for. In this work, we measure the counterfactual effect of effort levels on both proprietary and open-weight models on four benchmarks, spanning single-call reasoning and long-horizon agentic tasks. Effort, it turns out, does not consistently preserve tasks that lower effort solves, can overspend, and can change trajectory behavior. First, we find that although higher effort can raise accuracy in aggregate, it frequently fails on tasks that lower effort solved. Second, on the subset of easy tasks that every effort level solves, higher effort is generally more costly, with the highest effort level costing up to 42 as much as the lowest. Lastly, effort changes not only how many tokens are used, but also how agents solve tasks: at higher effort, agents verify their own work more heavily and more often search for solutions than work through problems directly, both of which lengthen trajectories without necessarily altering the outcome. These findings show that naively choosing the highest level is not only cost-inefficient but also offers no guarantee of the best instancelevel performance. simple alternative, starting at the lowest effort and escalating only after verified failure, matches or improves on fixed top effort across all models and benchmarks"

Across nine frontier agent systems and 2,151 scored rollouts, GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5.5 rank first and second with both the judges and human annotators, and the strongest system satisfies every core requirement on 76.3% of tasks. Delivery is second axis: counting missing videos as zero changes the ranking, and three quarters of the lost videos are final renders that started too late, ran too slowly or never started, failure of how agents plan their hour. Agents build their own rendering pipelines and inspect their own frames, yet how much they look does not predict quality. The judges ranking of the systems closely matches blind human preferences. 1. Introduction Video generation has advanced rapidly through diffusion transformers [7, 21, 32, 45, 51, 56], and benchmarks such as VBench [27, 59] and EvalCrafter [36] measure how plausible and prompt-aligned the generated clips look. Yet many of the videos people actually makeclassroom lessons, paper explainers, data stories, product walkthroughs, assembly sequences, rhythm gamesdemand properties that pixel generators do not guarantee: exact text and numbers, rule-consistent state changes, multi-minute structure, sound aligned to events, and an artifact that can be revised. These are natural properties of programs. Humans already make such videos with Manim or Remotion, and coding agents such as Claude Code and Codex [1, 41] can now work autonomously for hours, driving full toolchaina renderer, headless browser, TTS engine, ffmpegon their own. Users have begun to ask agents for finished videos this way, which requires multimodal agent that reads input assets, produces picture and sound through code, and inspects its own renders to correct them. How good are agents at making videos? Existing evaluations do not answer this. Video-generation benchmarks score short, silent clips from generative models [24, 27, 36]. Codeto-visual benchmarks score single programs or static artifacts such as charts, web pages and SVGs [11, 48, 49, 58]. Codebased lesson and paper-to-video systems [12, 33, 63] are language-model pipelines: workflow designed by hand for one domain and one renderer, in which model is called at preset steps; they do not test general agents. Agentic benchmarks [30, 38, 54] verify textual or functional outcomes with unit tests, whereas video is perceptual, temporal and audio-visual outcome for which no unit test exists. Video therefore tests what text-only agentic benchmarks cannot: whether an agent can check its own output across picture, sound and time. We present VibeVideoBench (Figs. 1 and 2), benchmark that evaluates multimodal agents end to end on codedriven video generation: given specification and optional input assets, an agent must deliver finished MP4, built entirely with code, inside sandbox within one hour, with narration, music and sound effects as each tasks audio policy specifies. Every frame is rendered by code: no image or video generation model is available, and the only generative tool is TTS. Its 239 tasks span 12 categories, from data stories and music-driven pieces to classroom lessons and paper explainers, in English and Chinese. Three design choices shape it. Tasks must be realistic yet checkable. Real requests are open-ended, vague and often missing the content they refer to, so they cannot be checked as written. We build tasks from four sourcesreal user requests to code-video tools (rewritten), real videos (seeded from their dense captions), real asset packs such as 3D scans and game levels, and lecture notes and research papersand rewrite each into self-contained specification that fixes facts, identities, stages, timing and the audio policy while preserving creative freedom. Evaluation must rest on evidence in the video. video has no unit test, holistic score cannot be traced to evidence, judge may credit result the video merely claims, and many VLMs cannot hear. Evaluation therefore has three layers with different exec"

Abstract ChronoReplay mitigates interface lock-in in spatial vision-language models by replaying heterogeneous spatial supervision chronologically, improving cross-task transfer and enabling efficient inference-time evidence selection.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Training a spatial VLM to answer which side an object lies on does not require it to preserve the metric displacement that determines that answer, while supervision for a distance estimate may leave categorical relations largely unconstrained. When post-training emphasizes one readout, improvements can therefore concentrate on that interface while transfer to others remains weak or even degrades; we call this observed specialization interface lock-in. ChronoReplay addresses it by replaying heterogeneous spatial supervision as chronologically ordered episodes, retaining source-native targets, and balancing supervision across families. Under the same observation access, a single ChronoReplay checkpoint improves OVO-S-Bench, OST-Bench, and UCS-Bench together, including a 13.41-point gain on OST-Bench, and achieves the highest three-benchmark mean among the supervision settings compared here. Same-data controls further separate the ingredients of this transfer: answer serialization redistributes gains across OST and UCS, family-balanced learning improves over ordinary joint SFT and natural turn weighting, and chronological replay exceeds a shifted-order control by about 2.41 OST points across two paired seeds. Adaptive frame selection then changes evidence access while keeping the trained model fixed, providing an additional inference-time gain at lower visual cost. Streaming spatial performance depends on what distinctions supervision preserves, how heterogeneous histories are organized during learning, and what causal evidence inference makes available at test time.
[28.10.2026 06:37] Response: {"desc": "Статья показывает, что при дообучении пространственных VLM (vision-language models) модель часто улучшается только по одному типу вопросов, например по направлению объекта, и почти не переносит навык на другие, что авторы называют interface lock-in. Предлагается метод ChronoReplay, который воспроизводит разнородные пространственные данные в хронологическом порядке эпизодов, сохраняет исходные целевые метки и балансирует supervision между семействами задач. Единый checkpoint, обученный так, улучшает сразу несколько бенчмарков, включая OVO-S-Bench, OST-Bench и UCS-Bench, с приростом 13.41 на OST-Bench. Дополнительно адаптивный отбор кадров на этапе inference даёт прирост качества при меньших вычислительных затратах на визуальную часть.", "emoji": "🧭", "title": "Учим модель помнить пространство во времени, а не в одном ответе"}

Abstract Hy-Nomas is a video world model that uses causal attention and structured captions to follow multi-turn text and camera instructions while preserving interaction history.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Interactive world generation requires models to follow successive commands while preserving relevant history and action consequences. We present Hy-Nomas, a video world model with an 8B diffusion backbone that supports free-form text instructions and continuous camera control across multiple interaction turns. It generates variable-length segments conditioned on current inputs and cached history. Our central design aligns structured video captions with action-aware causal attention: descriptive context remains accessible across segments, while action instructions are restricted to their intended temporal windows. A vision-language Prompt Enhancer (PE) translates user requests and multimodal interaction histories into structured conditions for the next segment. A multi-stage curriculum progressively introduces causal generation, text interaction, camera control, and training on model-generated histories, followed by diffusion distillation. In human evaluation, Hy-Nomas shows superior performance over existing world models. We release model weights, code and benchmarks, together with a lightweight adaptation workflow, to support reproducible research on interactive world generation.
[28.10.2026 06:38] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлена Hy-Nomas — модель мира для видео (video world model) с 8B диффузионным бэкбоном, которая выполняет многоходовые текстовые инструкции и непрерывное управление камерой. Ключевая идея — согласование структурированных описаний видео с каузальным вниманием (causal attention) с учётом действий: описательный контекст доступен между сегментами, а инструкции действий ограничены своими временными окнами. Модуль Prompt Enhancer на основе vision-language модели переводит запросы пользователя и мультимодальную историю взаимодействия в структурированные условия для следующего сегмента. Обучение проходит по многоэтапному curriculum, завершается дистилляцией диффузии, и модель превосходит существующие world models по оценке людей.", "emoji": "🎬", "title": "Интерактивный мир из видео, который помнит историю и слушает команды"}

Abstract Rollback improves robot policies by using world models to rewind to past decisions, explore counterfactual actions, and generate fine-grained supervision, while continuously refining dynamics via real-world feedback.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Action-conditioned world models offer a scalable alternative to costly real-world interaction for improving robot policies. However, existing world-model-based methods largely treat world models as forward-only surrogates of the physical environment, leaving untapped a capability no physical environment affords: rewinding to past decision points to explore counterfactual futures. We introduce Rollback, a policy improvement paradigm that turns world models from passive simulators into counterfactual engines: it rolls back to critical decision points, branches into alternative actions, and contrasts their outcomes to attribute success to individual decisions. This concentrates imagination where it matters most and distills counterfactual outcomes into fine-grained, action-level supervision without a separately learned value model. We build a scalable training system that unifies distributed world-model search with asynchronous real-robot execution, forming a self-reinforcing cycle: real-world experience continually sharpens the world model, and a sharper world model in turn drives further policy improvement. We term the world-model side of this cycle test-time physics grounding, a capability that hand-built physics simulators lack: while the world model serves as the training environment, real transitions from the policy it trains correct its dynamics through a lightweight LoRA pipeline. Experiments on three challenging real-world tasks show that Rollback improves success rates over the strongest baseline by an absolute 10.67%, 11.11%, and 11.85% on block extraction, ball tossing, and tabletop curling, respectively, while achieving more stable improvement throughout training. Although initialized from teleoperated demonstrations far below human level, Rollback surpasses the average success rate of human players on both ball tossing and tabletop curling within 8 hours of training.

Система откатывается к критическим точкам принятия решений, пробует альтернативные действия и сравнивает их исходы, что позволяет присвоить успех отдельным решениям без отдельной value model. Модель мира непрерывно уточняется реальными переходами через lightweight LoRA, что реализует идею test-time physics grounding. Abstract ThinkWorld improves robotic generalization by representing actions as dynamics chains and using world imagination to learn from unlabeled human videos.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have made substantial progress in robotic manipulation, but their generalization remains constrained by the diversity and coverage of action-labeled robot demonstrations. Human videos provide much broader behavioral and environmental experience, while learning from them faces a fundamental trade-off between scalable data and explicit action grounding. We introduce ThinkWorld, a VLA learning framework that grounds action reasoning in world dynamics and strengthens it through reinforcement learning with world imagination. ThinkWorld represents each action thought as a Chain-of-Dynamics (CoD), a sequence of discrete motion tokens that captures the intermediate dynamics connecting the current world to a future state and serves as a common action representation across embodiments. We first initialize CoD with action-labeled demonstrations, then predict the future induced by each CoD and reinforce those whose predictions better match the observed outcome. This enables videos without action annotations to provide direct supervision from observed state transitions, allowing human and robot experience to refine the same reasoning space. Experiments in simulation and on real robots demonstrate effective human-to-robot transfer and strong generalization to unseen tasks and environments.

Модель сначала обучается на демонстрациях с разметкой действий, затем с помощью reinforcement learning с world imagination усиливает те CoD, которые лучше предсказывают наблюдаемый исход. Abstract Executable code representations of video content improve reasoning and reconstruction over natural language, yet current multimodal models still struggle to generate faithful code captions for complex temporal dynamics.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Video captioning aims to preserve visual information through compact descriptions, predominantly using natural language. However, precisely expressing fine-grained spatial structures and temporal dynamics in natural language often leads to lengthy and potentially ambiguous descriptions. In this work, we explore Code as Video Caption, a paradigm where executable programs serve as a complementary representation of visual content through graphical primitives, layout rules, and functions of time. To examine whether code captions effectively preserve video information, we compare them with natural language captions on downstream question answering and video reconstruction tasks. Our results show that code captions achieve superior reasoning accuracy and reconstruction fidelity. Despite these advantages, realizing the potential of code captioning depends on whether current multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can generate faithful code captions. Abstract Large-scale evaluation reveals that dedicated memory systems often underperform naive retrieval-augmented generation, introducing a framework of memory benefit and memory tax to assess net value.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Memory is increasingly used in long-horizon agents, yet the net value of building memory over direct retrieval from raw history remains largely unclear. Through a large-scale evaluation across multiple benchmarks and model backbones, we find many dedicated memory systems underperform naive RAG in many cases, indicating a negative net value. Taking RAG as a reference, we dissect the net value of memory into the gains and losses over direct retrieval, termed memory benefit and memory tax. These gains and losses arise because memory process changes how the raw information is organized and accessed. We view such changes as the double-edged deviations from the raw history. They are originally designed and introduced to benefit the task, for example by reorganizing facts or distilling preferences, but some may also remove or obscure evidence, giving rise to memory tax. Since memory benefit reflects the expected gains of its design, we then focus more on the overlooked memory tax, study how it arises from the system and evaluation side, and generalize the concept to more application scenarios of memory. We hope the notion of memory tax can provide a new lens and shift more attention from standalone performance to the net value of memory, informing more reliable memory designs and evaluations. We will open-source the code and results to the community. Project Page: https://hexuan21.github.io/MemoryTax/

Оценка проводилась на нескольких бенчмарках и разных backbone-моделях, а основное внимание уделено изучению memory tax, его источников и обобщению на другие сценарии. Abstract LociArt enables precise local video stylization with region-style control and temporal consistency via a generative refinement network, automated dataset construction, and a dedicated benchmark.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Video stylization typically transforms entire scenes, while local artistic editing requires precise region--style association, spatial control, and temporal consistency. We introduce LociArt, a unified paradigm for free-style local video stylization that explicitly specifies where to stylize and what style to apply while preserving non-target content. At its core, GRNStyle builds on Generative Refinement Networks (GRN) and introduces a polar style-aware mask that embeds style identities as equal-norm directions within selected regions. Complementary representation--geometry conditioning combines encoded mask features with raw-mask spatial support, integrating spatiotemporal context with geometric control. To provide paired supervision, we develop an automated pipeline combining global stylization, video object removal, matting, and compositing, and construct the LociArt-100K Dataset with 100K local stylization pairs. We further establish the LociArt Benchmark, comprising 80 source videos and 84 target masks covering 168 foreground/background stylization cases, with a Style Prototype Similarity (SPS) evaluation scheme for regional style quality and complementary measures of non-target preservation and temporal consistency. Experiments show that GRNStyle outperforms existing methods in regional style quality and spatial control while maintaining strong temporal consistency. Qualitative examples further illustrate joint stylization of multiple regions with the same or different learned styles, despite training only on single-region, single-style pairs. Together, these components provide a unified foundation for learning and evaluating controllable local video stylization. Project page: https://higherhu.github.io/lociart/.

Ядро метода GRNStyle построено на Generative Refinement Networks (GRN) и использует полярную style-aware маску, кодирующую стили как направления равной нормы внутри выбранных областей, плюс условие на представление и геометрию. At its core, GRNStyle builds on Generative Refinement Networks (GRN) and introduces a polar style-aware mask that embeds style identities as equal-norm directions within selected regions. Complementary representation--geometry conditioning combines encoded mask features with raw-mask spatial support, integrating spatiotemporal context with geometric control. To provide paired supervision, we develop an automated pipeline combining global stylization, video object removal, matting, and compositing, and construct the LociArt-100K Dataset with 100K local stylization pairs. We further establish the LociArt Benchmark, comprising 80 source videos and 84 target masks covering 168 foreground/background stylization cases, with a Style Prototype Similarity (SPS) evaluation scheme for regional style quality and complementary measures of non-target preservation and temporal consistency. Experiments show that GRNStyle outperforms existing methods in regional style quality and spatial control while maintaining strong temporal consistency. Qualitative examples further illustrate joint stylization of multiple regions with the same or different learned styles, despite training only on single-region, single-style pairs. Together, these components provide a unified foundation for learning and evaluating controllable local video stylization. Project page: https://higherhu.github.io/lociart/."

Experiments show that GRNStyle outperforms existing methods in regional style quality and spatial control while maintaining strong temporal consistency. Qualitative examples further illustrate joint stylization of multiple regions with the same or different learned styles, despite training only on single-region, single-style pairs. Together, these components provide a unified foundation for learning and evaluating controllable local video stylization. Project page: https://higherhu.github.io/lociart/."

Abstract HABIT-Bench evaluates whether LLM agent memory systems can infer implicit user habits from sparse dialogue evidence and apply them correctly across long-term interactions.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small In long-term interactions with large language model (LLM) agents, users reveal habits through their choices and actions. These habits capture implicit user information that agents must infer across conversations. However, individual observations may provide only partial evidence about a habit or when it applies. Retaining past interactions alone does not ensure that agents can infer habits and apply them appropriately. We introduce HABIT-Bench to evaluate how well agent memory systems infer habits from weak evidence and apply them to later requests. We construct synthetic user histories from task-oriented dialogue data and controlled habit graphs. The histories span hundreds of sessions across food, finance, software, and travel. Four-choice tasks assess agents' ability to infer user habits and apply them appropriately. They include temporary exceptions, changes in habits, and suggestions that users have not accepted. Sampled tasks undergo independent human review. We evaluate eight memory methods with Qwen3-235B-A22B and GLM-5.3-Flash. The best-performing memory method achieves 33.61\% macro accuracy across the four domains. Performance remains limited even when annotated evidence is supplied directly. These results expose a persistent gap between access to past interactions and the ability to infer and appropriately use user habits.

Синтетические истории пользователей строятся на основе датасетов task-oriented диалогов и контролируемых графов привычек в областях еды, финансов, ПО и путешествий. Abstract The Trinity framework recursively improves multimodal agents by organizing tasks into a capability tree, generating simulated training worlds, and routing specialized harness modules to boost performance on diverse benchmarks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Multimodal intelligence can be viewed as human-guided co-evolution between worlds and agents through interaction. However, scaling this process faces two bottlenecks: constructing heterogeneous, verifiable environments is costly, and manual Harness iteration cannot keep pace with growing task diversity and complexity. A key insight is that tasks with different modalities and surface forms can share the same underlying capability requirements. We therefore introduce The Trinity, a recursive self-improvement framework coupling world generation with Harness evolution through three roles: Creator, Explorer, and Evolver. Drawing on verified execution trajectories, Evolver shifts the basis of task organization from surface categories to shared requirements, reorganizing the original task tree into a hierarchical capability tree. We introduce Capability-Driven Real2Sim2Real: for heterogeneous tasks at the same node, Creator abstracts their common requirements into simplified, verifiable simulated worlds for capability learning, while evaluation on original tasks measures transfer. Along this capability tree, Explorer progressively evolves reusable capability modules through selective inheritance, forming a Harness tree whose validated updates accumulate across iterations within a fixed topology. The resulting Harnesses specialize in different capabilities, motivating a Capability-Aware Harness Router that uses public task information to select compatible module configurations from the tree. Our evolved Harnesses improve mean performance by 34 points across 28 training benchmarks and 26 points across 18 unseen benchmarks, including two real-world embodied tasks.
[28.10.2026 06:39] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен фреймворк Trinity, который рекурсивно улучшает мультимодальных AI-агентов: задачи организуются в дерево способностей, а Creator генерирует упрощённые верифицируемые симулированные миры для обучения. Роль Explorer постепенно эволюционирует переиспользуемые модули (Harness tree), а Capability-Aware Harness Router выбирает подходящую конфигурацию модулей по публичной информации о задаче. Подход основан на идее, что задачи разных модальностей могут требовать одних и тех же базовых способностей. Эксперименты показывают прирост средней производительности на 34 пункта на обучающих бенчмарках и на 26 пунктов на невидимых, включая две реальные embodied-задачи.", "emoji": "🌳", "title": "Общие способности важнее формы задачи: агенты учатся, переиспользуя навыки"}

Abstract PDRVLA improves robot action generation by integrating a lightweight physical dynamics module that predicts future visual features with world-model supervision, avoiding online video generation latency.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Vision--Language--Action (VLA) models generate robot actions from visual observations and language instructions, but their understanding of physical dynamics remains limited. Video world models can capture scene dynamics, yet generating future videos introduces high latency into the control loop. We introduce PDRVLA, whose lightweight Physical Dynamics Module extracts physical tokens from vision--language features to condition action generation without online video generation. The module learns through direct prediction of future RGB, depth, and optical-flow features, with auxiliary supervision through a frozen pretrained video world model. Training relies entirely on publicly available data, expanded through human-to-robot synthesis, subtask annotation, and embodied chain-of-thought (ECoT) annotation. Across four simulation benchmarks, PDRVLA achieves success rates of 99.3% on LIBERO, 89.9% on LIBERO-Plus, 93.6% on RoboTwin~2.0, and 81.5% on SimplerEnv. Ablations show improved future prediction with world-model supervision and evaluate how subtask conditioning, future object-pose supervision, and ECoT training affect action success.

Abstract Test-Time Memory Evolution updates external memory without changing model weights to guide scientific discovery more efficiently than test-time training.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Recent test-time training (TTT) frameworks have enabled open-weight large language models (LLMs) to outperform inference-only frameworks powered by frontier models on scientific discovery tasks. However, TTT introduces additional computational cost for online weight updates during the discovery process, making computational efficiency an important consideration when scaling to larger models. This motivates us to explore an efficient test-time adaptation method for scientific discovery. We introduce Test-Time Memory Evolution (TTME), a plug-and-play test-time adaptation method, as an alternative to TTT. TTME continually updates the external memory by distilling the experience to guide subsequent discovery without updating model weights. To have a clean comparison, we evaluate TTME and TTT under the same discovery framework, replacing only the test-time adaptation method. Our evaluation shows that TTME outperforms TTT on all five mathematical tasks, while reducing computational cost by 82.8-84.6% in FLOPs and 47.7-58.4% in runtime. These results establish TTME as an effective and computationally efficient alternative to TTT in the evaluated discovery settings.

TTME не меняет веса модели, а постоянно обновляет внешнюю память, дистиллируя накопленный опыт для направления последующего поиска. Сравнение проводилось в одной и той же среде открытий, где менялся только метод адаптации на этапе инференса. На пяти математических задачах TTME превзошёл TTT, снизив вычислительные затраты на 82.8–84.6% по FLOPs и на 47.7–58.4% по времени выполнения.", "emoji": "🧠", "title": "Память вместо весов: эффективная адаптация LLM при поиске научных открытий"}

Abstract MatAnyone-E accelerates high-resolution video matting by selectively applying high-resolution matching to uncertain regions and using a lightweight decoder, achieving large speedups with preserved quality.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small High-resolution video matting requires preserving fine details, such as hair strands and soft transparency, while maintaining consistency across frames. Memory-based methods achieve strong matting quality by matching current-frame features with information stored from earlier frames, but this process becomes increasingly costly as resolution grows. We present MatAnyone-E, an efficient extension of MatAnyone 2 that focuses computation where it is most needed. Our approach first performs low-resolution propagation to identify uncertain regions that require closer inspection and the memory candidates most relevant to them. It then performs high-resolution matching only for these regions using the selected memory, while reusing low-resolution matching results elsewhere. This strategy reduces both the number of locations requiring detailed matching and the amount of memory searched, while preserving the information needed to produce a complete matte. We further reduce the cost of generating high-resolution mattes with a lightweight decoder designed around measured runtime bottlenecks and trained to reproduce the original decoder's representations. Experiments show that MatAnyone-E maintains matting quality comparable to MatAnyone 2 while achieving an estimated 7.4x inference speedup and reducing peak GPU memory usage to 7.6% of the usage of MatAnyone 2 at 4K. With compilation-enabled inference on a single NVIDIA H200 GPU, it reaches 73.07 FPS at 1080p and 28.62 FPS at 4K.
[28.10.2026 06:39] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет MatAnyone-E — ускоренную версию модели для высокопроизводительного матинга видео, которая сохраняет тонкие детали, такие как пряди волос и полупрозрачность. Метод сначала выполняет распространение на низком разрешении, чтобы найти неопределённые области и релевантные элементы памяти, а затем применяет высокоразрешающее сопоставление только к этим участкам. Paper text to classify:

"Abstract MatAnyone-E accelerates high-resolution video matting by selectively applying high-resolution matching to uncertain regions and using a lightweight decoder, achieving large speedups with preserved quality.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small High-resolution video matting requires preserving fine details, such as hair strands and soft transparency, while maintaining consistency across frames. Memory-based methods achieve strong matting quality by matching current-frame features with information stored from earlier frames, but this process becomes increasingly costly as resolution grows. We present MatAnyone-E, an efficient extension of MatAnyone 2 that focuses computation where it is most needed. Our approach first performs low-resolution propagation to identify uncertain regions that require closer inspection and the memory candidates most relevant to them. It then performs high-resolution matching only for these regions using the selected memory, while reusing low-resolution matching results elsewhere. This strategy reduces both the number of locations requiring detailed matching and the amount of memory searched, while preserving the information needed to produce a complete matte. We further reduce the cost of generating high-resolution mattes with a lightweight decoder designed around measured runtime bottlenecks and trained to reproduce the original decoder's representations. Experiments show that MatAnyone-E maintains matting quality comparable to MatAnyone 2 while achieving an estimated 7.4x inference speedup and reducing peak GPU memory usage to 7.6% of the usage of MatAnyone 2 at 4K. With compilation-enabled inference on a single NVIDIA H200 GPU, it reaches 73.07 FPS at 1080p and 28.62 FPS at 4K."

Abstract RSI Arena demonstrates that autonomous AI agents can independently train and iteratively improve language models using human feedback, with most agents achieving preference gains in a second stage despite modest benchmark improvements.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Can AI agents autonomously train useful language models and improve them using feedback from users? We introduce RSI Arena, a two-stage study of autonomous post-training as a step toward recursive self-improvement. Ten frontier models act as training agents, each starting from NVIDIA Nemotron 3.5 Lightning 30B-A3B Base. During a six-day first stage, each agent has 1,000 GPU-hours and $300 in API credit on a shared 64-GPU cluster, independently selecting data, implementing training procedures, and choosing checkpoints. We evaluate their models on 20 benchmarks and through blind human evaluations. All ten agents produce working assistants, with the strongest model, trained by GPT-6 Astra, closing a median of 76% of the base-to-Instruct gap across 12 capability benchmarks. Its human-preference rating is also close to NVIDIA Instruct after statistical adjustment for answer length and formatting. In Stage 2, each agent receives the comparison records and votes involving its Stage 1 model and is given three additional days for further training. Using NVIDIA Instruct as a common reference across the two evaluation rounds, we observe an improvement from Stage 1 to Stage 2 in the style-adjusted relative rating of eight of the ten models. We show that benchmark gains were modest, with no clear link to preference gains, while some agents encouraged longer responses and one model showed increased harmful compliance on StrongREJECT. RSI Arena provides an auditable setting for studying how human feedback can guide autonomous model development. It takes a step toward recursive self-improvement with human feedback (RSIHF), in which people help define what improvement means for the AI systems they use.

Abstract Omni-InteractScope evaluates continuous audiovisual interaction reliability, revealing that current Omni-LLMs struggle with timely tool use, query-free reminders, and sustained guidance.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Omnimodal large language models (Omni-LLMs) are increasingly expected to assist users while observing continuous audiovisual streams. However, existing evaluations provide limited insight into how reliably these models connect stream evidence and user needs to timely, sustained interventions. We introduce Omni-InteractScope, a benchmark of 1,479 human-annotated, cross-reviewed interactions with 1,790 temporal targets across eight task groups. It links input modalities, event triggers, intervention types, and user needs, covering event-timed tool invocation and query-free assistance alongside notifications, warnings, state tracking, and continuous responses. Our experimental results on the proposed benchmark reveal three critical limitations of current Omni-LLMs: (1) Timely tool invocation remains unreliable: all 12 evaluated models produce an on-time first response for at most 40\% of tool-invocation targets, and joint timing-and-tool accuracy reaches at most 32.0\%. (2) Query-free reminders remain poorly covered: all evaluated models achieve lower timely reference-target recall on query-free reminders than on explicit questions, measured on their respective target sets. Reminder recall never exceeds 23.0\%. (3) End-to-end guidance coverage remains limited: nine models cover none of the 26 guidance sequences in full, and the remaining three cover only one to three sequences each. Targeted controls further show that tool-invocation scores alone do not establish event grounding and that reminder coverage depends on response history and frequency. These findings motivate developing and evaluating streaming assistants for timely action, initiative under general user needs, and coverage of entire response sequences.

(2) Query-free reminders remain poorly covered: all evaluated models achieve lower timely reference-target recall on query-free reminders than on explicit questions, measured on their respective target sets. Reminder recall never exceeds 23.0\%. (3) End-to-end guidance coverage remains limited: nine models cover none of the 26 guidance sequences in full, and the remaining three cover only one to three sequences each. Targeted controls further show that tool-invocation scores alone do not establish event grounding and that reminder coverage depends on response history and frequency. These findings motivate developing and evaluating streaming assistants for timely action, initiative under general user needs, and coverage of entire response sequences."

Abstract Cross-modal steering vectors transfer between text and image generation in unified multimodal models, revealing shared causal control mechanisms despite a modality gap.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Are concepts and mechanisms unified across modalities in current AI models, or instead implemented separately for each one? We operationalize this broad question of modality unification by studying cross-modal steering: 1) finding vectors that successfully steer generation on tokens of one modality, which then 2) transfer zero-shot to steer generation when added to tokens of another modality. Our study goes beyond simple correlational alignment (e.g. representational similarity) because steering is fundamentally causal. We study cross-modal steering in several unified multimodal models (UMMs), since they possess shared parameters for language and image generation, focusing on these two modalities. Across diverse steered concepts such as emotion and object size, we show that steering vectors transfer zero-shot in both directions (text-to-image & image-to-text). Even for concepts that are highly unique to one modality (e.g. formal vs. colloquial language), steering vectors often induce a consistent, interpretable effect in the other modality, such as formal clothing and design. This transfer has a geometric signature too: despite a clear modality gap separating text and image representations, steering vectors with strong cross-modal transferability tend to point in similar directions for the same concept. Our findings shed light on how vision and language interact inside unified multimodal models and point towards more general, modality-agnostic approaches for controlling their behavior.
[28.10.2026 06:39] Response: {"desc": "Авторы исследуют, объединены ли концепции между текстом и изображениями внутри унифицированных мультимодальных моделей (UMM) или реализованы отдельно для каждой модальности. Они используют cross-modal steering: вектор, найденный для управления генерацией одной модальности, добавляется к токенам другой модальности и переносится zero-shot без дополнительного обучения. Эксперименты показывают, что такие векторы работают в обоих направлениях (текст→изображение и изображение→текст), например, для эмоций и размера объектов. Геометрически сильные переносимые векторы направлены похоже, несмотря на заметный modality gap между представлениями текста и изображений.", "emoji": "🧭", "title": "Общие причинно-следственные механизмы управления текстом и изображениями в одной модели"}

Prompt: Read an abstract of the ML paper and return a JSON with fields: 'desc': explanation of the paper in Russian (4 sentences), use correct machine learning terms. Abstract Under KL-regularized control, a policy encodes its harness preferences, so deployed performance equals harness potential minus policy mismatch, and harness comparisons must separate these terms to predict training gains.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Language model agents are post-trained and deployed through a harness, the prompts, tools, and control logic that present a task to the model and execute its outputs. Harnesses are often revised and judged by how an already trained model performs under them, which assumes that what the model learned under one harness carries over to the next. We ask what a policy learns from its harness and what survives when the harness changes. Under KL-regularized control, we show that the log odds of an optimal policy recover how strongly its harness favors each action over the others, so a trained policy carries the preferences of its harness. Deployed return then equals the ceiling of the harness, the best return it offers, minus the mismatch of the policy, the return lost to its own suboptimal decisions. Training changes only the mismatch, while changing the harness moves both terms. Comparing harnesses with a fixed model therefore mixes what each harness offers with how well that model uses it. In MiniWoB++, post-training makes a model's preferences specific to its training harness. Reversing the order of the observation fields leaves every optimal decision unchanged yet lowers a trained model's success from 85.0% to 19.2%. Conversely, every new harness that a fixed-model comparison rejects but that raises the ceiling outperforms the original agent after further training under it, and ceiling and mismatch measured beforehand predict these gains. For coding-agent harnesses on Terminal-Bench Lite, ceiling minus mismatch estimated with a stronger model as a stand-in for the optimum reproduces the accuracy ranking, while either term alone does not. Code is available at https://github.com/2020-qqtcg/HRP.
[28.10.2026 06:39] Response: {"desc": "Статья показывает, что при KL-регуляризованном управлении политика языковой модели (LLM) запоминает предпочтения своего harness, то есть набора промптов, инструментов и логики управления. Итоговая отдача равна потолку harness минус рассогласование политики, то есть потерям из-за её собственных неоптимальных решений. При дообучении меняется только рассогласование, а смена harness влияет на оба слагаемых. Поэтому сравнение harness при фиксированной модели смешивает качество самого harness и умение модели им пользоваться, и предварительно измеренные потолок и рассогласование позволяют предсказать выигрыш от дообучения.", "emoji": "🧭", "title": "Модель учится не только задаче, но и среде: сравнивайте harness отдельно от политики"}

[28.10.2026 06:39] Claude request. Model: claude-haiku-5-5. Prompt: Read an abstract of the ML paper and return a JSON with fields: 'desc': explanation of the paper in Russian (4 sentences), use correct machine learning terms. 'emoji': emoji that will reflect the theme of an article somehow, only one emoji. 'title': a slogan of a main idea of the article in Russian. Return only JSON and nothing else.

Abstract Reasoning effort in generative models acts as a credence good where higher compute does not guarantee better instance-level results, and an escalation strategy from low effort improves cost-efficiency.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Generative models increasingly expose a reasoning effort setting that lets users scale inference-time compute, trading higher cost for potentially improved performance. Users must choose an effort level before knowing whether extra reasoning will help, and even a successful run does not reveal whether the effort purchased was necessary. Reasoning effort is therefore a credence good: buyers observe the outcome but cannot assess the marginal value of the computation they paid for. In this work, we measure the counterfactual effect of effort levels on both proprietary and open-weight models on four benchmarks, spanning single-call reasoning and long-horizon agentic tasks. Effort, it turns out, does not consistently preserve tasks that lower effort solves, can overspend, and can change trajectory behavior. First, we find that although higher effort can raise accuracy in aggregate, it frequently fails on tasks that lower effort solved. Second, on the subset of easy tasks that every effort level solves, higher effort is generally more costly, with the highest effort level costing up to 42x as much as the lowest. Lastly, effort changes not only how many tokens are used, but also how agents solve tasks: at higher effort, agents verify their own work more heavily and more often search for solutions than work through problems directly, both of which lengthen trajectories without necessarily altering the outcome. These findings show that naively choosing the highest level is not only cost-inefficient but also offers no guarantee of the best instance-level performance. A simple alternative, starting at the lowest effort and escalating only after a verified failure, matches or improves on fixed top effort across all models and benchmarks at lower cost for most proprietary models.

Авторы показывают, что более высокий effort не гарантирует лучший результат на уровне отдельных задач: модель может провалить задачи, которые решались при низком effort, и переплачивает за простые примеры до 42 раз. Также высокий effort меняет поведение агентов, заставляя их чаще проверять себя и искать решения, что удлиняет траектории без улучшения результата. Предложенная стратегия эскалации, начиная с минимального effort и повышая его только после подтвержденной ошибки, сопоставима или лучше фиксированного максимального effort и дешевле для большинства проприетарных LLM.", "emoji": "💸", "title": "Больше вычислений не значит лучше: начинайте с малого и эскалируйте только при ошибке"}

Abstract The study introduces a benchmark and metric to evaluate whether robot navigation policies adapt continuous motion to obstacle safety semantics, revealing a gap in translating semantic caution into safe movement.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Safe household navigation requires robots to regulate clearance and speed according to what an obstacle is and how close it is, without unnecessary conservatism. Yet existing evaluations do not test whether semantic safety distinctions are reflected in continuous motion. To that end, we introduce OSMA-Bench, an Obstacle-Conditioned Safe Motion Adaptation Benchmark built on spatially matched scenario sets that differ only in obstacle type. We also propose the Semantic Sensitivity Index (SSI), which measures whether clearance and distance-dependent speed and smoothness follow the caution ordering of obstacles. Evaluating VLM-based and vision-language-action (VLA) policies on the benchmark, we find that neither consistently adapts its motion to obstacle safety, even with explicit safety prompts. Our analysis shows that the policies can correctly distinguish obstacles by safety relevance but fail to reflect this distinction in the motion parameters or actions they produce. For VLM-based policies, which recognize the obstacle and then generate a navigation program, adding an explicit parameter-inference step between these two stages improves adaptation. These findings expose a gap in semantic-to-motion grounding, highlighting the need to translate semantic caution into appropriately safe motion.

Тестирование VLM-based и VLA-политик показало, что ни одна из них стабильно не меняет клиренс, скорость и плавность движения в зависимости от типа препятствия, даже при явных промптах о безопасности. Abstract Prox replaces calibrated thresholds with top-k channel selection and a weight-derived reader to accelerate Mixture-of-Experts inference while preserving accuracy.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models are bottlenecked by loading routed-expert weights from memory during interactive decoding at small batch sizes. Because most intermediate channels in a routed expert contribute little to any single token, channel selectors predict which channels matter and read only their weights, typically keeping channels above a threshold calibrated on offline data. We show that channel selection needs no calibration data. First, a calibrated threshold does not fix how much a selector reads, because the number of channels passing it varies across inputs; Prox with thresholds calibrated for an 18% read budget actually reads only 12.9% on average across our benchmarks. Second, top-k keeps each token's k highest-scoring channels and needs no calibration data. At the calibrated budget, a selector's top-k version matches or beats its threshold version on the same score in 40 of 43 comparisons at 13% and 18% budgets, including all 23 on generation tasks. The top-k versions also avoid the repetition-loop collapse that calibrated thresholds cause on Gemma 4. Calibrating a threshold thus provides neither of the two things selectors use it for: a known read budget and a consistent accuracy gain at that budget. We furthermore introduce a reader built entirely from the model's weights, which screens channels with a ternary sketch and recovers the skipped ones with a low-rank map. Across three post-trained MoE models, our reader retains 98.1% of full-model accuracy at 17.8% of routed-expert reads, with Triton kernels running MoE blocks 1.60 to 1.90x faster than optimized vLLM at batch one and up to 2.69x at larger batches on the same three models.
[28.10.2026 06:40] Response: {"desc": "В статье предлагается ускорить инференс Mixture-of-Experts моделей, где узким местом является загрузка весов маршрутизируемых экспертов из памяти при небольших батчах. Авторы показывают, что калибровка порога для выбора каналов не нужна: метод top-k, выбирающий k наиболее значимых каналов для каждого токена, не требует калибровочных данных и стабильно не хуже порогового подхода. Также вводится reader, построенный только по весам модели: он отбирает каналы с помощью тернарного sketch и восстанавливает пропущенные через low-rank отображение. Метод сохраняет 98.1% точности полной модели, читая лишь 17.8% весов экспертов, и ускоряет MoE-блоки в 1.6–2.69 раза по сравнению с оптимизированным vLLM.", "emoji": "⚡", "title": "Быстрее MoE без калибровки: читай только нужные каналы"}

Abstract LongChang evaluates whether LLMs can autonomously discover altered physical laws through simulated experimentation, finding that only one model achieved limited success.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Scientific discovery often begins with an unexpected observation that prompts a new research question. In a world with altered physical laws, can large language models (LLMs) notice unexpected behavior, such as heavier objects falling more slowly, and discover the law through experiments without being told what to investigate? To evaluate this ability, we introduce LongChang, an automated framework that creates simulated worlds with altered physics, where one or more laws differ from ordinary physics. The evaluated LLMs act as explorers, manipulating objects and building tools to investigate their own questions without research tasks or hints about these laws. Separate LLM agents generate and review scenes, review tool designs, and evaluate reports for correctness and experimental support using the altered physical laws and exploration records. These records guide scene evolution for further exploration, allowing the full cycle to run without human intervention. Among 12 LLMs, GPT-6-Astra achieves a discovery rate of 16.7%, while the other 11 score 0%. The exploration records reveal uninvestigated anomalies and only partial identification of the altered physical laws.

Abstract VibeVideoBench evaluates multimodal agents that generate videos via code, finding that top systems meet core requirements on most tasks but frequently fail due to poor time management.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Diffusion video models produce striking clips, but they cannot reliably render an exact formula, a correct chart, a rule-consistent game or narration synchronized to on-screen events. A different route is emerging: multimodal agents that write code to render every frame and synthesize every sound. We introduce VibeVideoBench, a benchmark for evaluating agents on code-driven video generation. Its 239 self-contained tasks in English and Chinese span 12 categories, from data stories and music-driven pieces to classroom lessons and paper explainers; each asks an agent, running in its native harness inside a sandbox with no image or video generation model, to deliver a finished MP4 with sound within one hour. Evaluation is hierarchical and evidence-based: program checks of the delivery spec, about 13k task-specific rubric checks traced to the requirements they enforce, and five quality dimensions anchored per task, scored by three VLM judge panels. Across nine frontier agent systems and 2,151 scored rollouts, GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5.5 rank first and second with both the judges and human annotators, and the strongest system satisfies every core requirement on 76.3% of tasks. Delivery is a second axis: counting missing videos as zero changes the ranking, and three quarters of the lost videos are final renders that started too late, ran too slowly or never started, a failure of how agents plan their hour. Agents build their own rendering pipelines and inspect their own frames, yet how much they look does not predict quality. The judges' ranking of the systems closely matches blind human preferences.

Paper text to classify:

"Abstract VibeVideoBench evaluates multimodal agents that generate videos via code, finding that top systems meet core requirements on most tasks but frequently fail due to poor time management.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Diffusion video models produce striking clips, but they cannot reliably render an exact formula, a correct chart, a rule-consistent game or narration synchronized to on-screen events. A different route is emerging: multimodal agents that write code to render every frame and synthesize every sound. We introduce VibeVideoBench, a benchmark for evaluating agents on code-driven video generation. Its 239 self-contained tasks in English and Chinese span 12 categories, from data stories and music-driven pieces to classroom lessons and paper explainers; each asks an agent, running in its native harness inside a sandbox with no image or video generation model, to deliver a finished MP4 with sound within one hour. Evaluation is hierarchical and evidence-based: program checks of the delivery spec, about 13k task-specific rubric checks traced to the requirements they enforce, and five quality dimensions anchored per task, scored by three VLM judge panels. Across nine frontier agent systems and 2,151 scored rollouts, GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5.5 rank first and second with both the judges and human annotators, and the strongest system satisfies every core requirement on 76.3% of tasks. Delivery is a second axis: counting missing videos as zero changes the ranking, and three quarters of the lost videos are final renders that started too late, ran too slowly or never started, a failure of how agents plan their hour. Agents build their own rendering pipelines and inspect their own frames, yet how much they look does not predict quality. The judges' ranking of the systems closely matches blind human preferences."

Abstract Just Action Model decouples backbone initialization, future observation objectives, and inference machinery to improve robot control with foresight training and reduced computation.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small The vision-language-action (VLA) and world-action model (WAM) communities debate whether control policies should inherit pretrained knowledge from vision-language models or from visual generators. The debate, however, bundles three separable choices: backbone initialization, the objective applied to future observations, and whether the machinery for that future loss is kept at inference. A gap between a VLA and a WAM therefore cannot simply be attributed to any one of them. We show that decoupling these choices yields a common framework for both families, which we term Just Action Model (JAM): a pretrained visual backbone with an action expert, trained with an action loss and a foresight loss. The foresight loss trains current-observation features to predict frozen encodings of the recorded future through a prediction head discarded after training, so no visual generation is involved in either training or inference, and the backbone can be chosen freely. We first compare objectives within fixed policy architectures, then evaluate the foresight recipe across backbone families. The resulting JAM policies outperform their matched FastWAM and ImageWAM counterparts on LIBERO and LIBERO-Plus, and achieve similar or slightly higher success rates on RoboTwin. Compared with FastWAM, JAM improves the success rates from 97.0%/55.3%/91.2% to 98.2%/64.5%/91.4%, respectively. With cached foresight targets, JAM reduces estimated online training FLOPs per sample per update by 73% relative to FastWAM. Our results show that future supervision improves control without visual generation or additional inference machinery, and strong foresight performance is attainable with either generator-initialized or vision-language backbones. Project Page: https://y0uroy.github.io/JAM
[28.10.2026 06:40] Response: {"desc": "Авторы разбирают спор между VLA и WAM моделями управления роботами и показывают, что он смешивает три независимых решения: инициализацию backbone, цель для будущих наблюдений и наличие механизма future loss на инференсе. Предложенная модель JAM использует предобученный визуальный backbone с action expert и обучается по двум функциям потерь: action loss и foresight loss. Foresight loss заставляет признаки текущего наблюдения предсказывать замороженные эмбеддинги будущего через prediction head, который отбрасывается после обучения, поэтому визуальная генерация не нужна ни при обучении, ни при инференсе. JAM превосходит аналогичные FastWAM и ImageWAM на LIBERO и LIBERO-Plus и сокращает вычисления на обучение примерно на 73%.", "emoji": "🤖", "title": "Предвидеть будущее без генерации: прогноз ради управления"}

Abstract OvisOCR3 is a compact 0.8B vision-language model that unifies diverse OCR tasks through multi-teacher distillation and reinforcement learning, achieving state-of-the-art results across document parsing, general OCR, key information extraction, figure captioning, and cross-page table reconstruction.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small We introduce OvisOCR3, a 0.8B model for a broad range of OCR-related tasks. OvisOCR3 unifies document parsing, general OCR, key information extraction (KIE), document figure captioning, and cross-page table reconstruction in a single model. We train the model on real and synthetic data through continued pretraining and use domain-balanced mid-training to balance supervision across tasks with different data volumes and response lengths. Multi-teacher on-policy distillation transfers expertise from five domain-specific 4B teachers into the shared model, followed by mixed-task reinforcement learning with domain-specific rewards. We compare OvisOCR3 with general-purpose vision-language models and specialized OCR models on public and in-house benchmarks covering all five task families. In these comparisons, it achieves an overall score of 96.74 on OmniDocBench v1.6 and the highest overall scores on PureDocBench, Real5-OmniDocBench, and MDPBench. On OCRBench v2, it ranks first, scoring 65.9 and 61.3 on the English and Chinese splits, respectively. It also outperforms the baselines on OCRBench and all four KIE benchmarks. On DocFigCapBench, it achieves the highest localization F1 and a caption score close to that of Qwen3.8-Max. For cross-page tables, it leads in Relation F1 on CrossTabBench and truncated-table TEDS on MPDocBench-Parse. These results demonstrate that a compact 0.8B model can achieve strong performance across diverse OCR-related tasks. OvisOCR3 is available at https://huggingface.co/ATH-MaaS/OvisOCR3.

"Abstract OvisOCR3 is a compact 0.8B vision-language model that unifies diverse OCR tasks through multi-teacher distillation and reinforcement learning, achieving state-of-the-art results across document parsing, general OCR, key information extraction, figure captioning, and cross-page table reconstruction.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small We introduce OvisOCR3, a 0.8B model for a broad range of OCR-related tasks. OvisOCR3 unifies document parsing, general OCR, key information extraction (KIE), document figure captioning, and cross-page table reconstruction in a single model. We train the model on real and synthetic data through continued pretraining and use domain-balanced mid-training to balance supervision across tasks with different data volumes and response lengths. Multi-teacher on-policy distillation transfers expertise from five domain-specific 4B teachers into the shared model, followed by mixed-task reinforcement learning with domain-specific rewards. We compare OvisOCR3 with general-purpose vision-language models and specialized OCR models on public and in-house benchmarks covering all five task families. In these comparisons, it achieves an overall score of 96.74 on OmniDocBench v1.6 and the highest overall scores on PureDocBench, Real5-OmniDocBench, and MDPBench. On OCRBench v2, it ranks first, scoring 65.9 and 61.3 on the English and Chinese splits, respectively. It also outperforms the baselines on OCRBench and all four KIE benchmarks. On DocFigCapBench, it achieves the highest localization F1 and a caption score close to that of Qwen3.8-Max. For cross-page tables, it leads in Relation F1 on CrossTabBench and truncated-table TEDS on MPDocBench-Parse. These results demonstrate that a compact 0.8B model can achieve strong performance across diverse OCR-related tasks. OvisOCR3 is available at https://huggingface.co/ATH-MaaS/OvisOCR3."

Abstract RynnWorld-Latent learns cross-embodiment world dynamics via 3D-grounded latent actions, enabling joint pretraining on diverse video sources and synthetic data generation for improved robot policy performance.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Learning controllable world dynamics from heterogeneous interaction videos is challenging because robot embodiments use incompatible action spaces, while human videos lack native control labels. We present RynnWorld-Latent, a cross-embodiment video world model that uses the 3D-grounded latent actions introduced by RynnLAM as a shared conditioning interface. This representation enables joint pretraining on human interaction videos, robot demonstrations, and simulation rollouts without requiring a common native action space. Built on a pretrained video transformer, RynnWorld-Latent combines global action tokens with an identity-initialized local pathway that aligns actions with future video latents. We pretrain on 23 datasets comprising approximately 553.5M frames and adapt the model to target robots through post-training. The framework supports two data-generation routes: digital teleoperation uses target robot commands to generate action-video demonstrations, while latent-action synthesis transfers source motion to an edited initial frame and pairs the resulting rollout with retargeted pseudo robot actions. Experiments show improved video prediction metrics on public benchmarks and two real-robot platforms, alongside qualitative action following when different action sequences share the same initial observation. Synthetic mid-training improves policy success rates on LIBERO and LIBERO-Plus, and both generation routes improve real-world policy success rates over real-demonstration-only training in the evaluated settings. These results support universal latent actions as a scalable interface for learning controllable visual dynamics across heterogeneous embodiments.
[28.10.2026 06:40] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлена RynnWorld-Latent — кросс-эмбодимент модель мира на основе видео, которая использует 3D-grounded латентные действия (RynnLAM) как общий интерфейс управления. Такой подход позволяет совместно предобучать модель на видео с участием людей, демонстрациях роботов и симуляциях без единого нативного пространства действий. Модель построена на предобученном video transformer и обучена на 23 датасетах (около 553,5M кадров), после чего адаптируется к целевым роботам через post-training. Эксперименты показывают улучшение метрик предсказания видео и успешности политик робота на LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus и двух реальных платформах, а синтетические данные повышают качество обучения.", "emoji": "🤖", "title": "Универсальные латентные действия — единый язык управления роботами"}

Abstract SearchHorizonBench evaluates how agents learn to estimate the value of additional search from execution history, showing that historical calibration improves prediction accuracy but leaves most search-gain variance unexplained.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small AI agents must decide when another search is worth its cost. Answer-success probabilities capture question difficulty, while this decision requires estimating the additional benefit of search. We introduce SearchHorizonBench, a benchmark for learning and evaluating search value from execution experience. Paired continuations and repeated executions separate expected search gain from generation noise and connect prediction quality to decision utility. The resource contains 45,632 executions across 3,168 questions. On 1,632 held-out questions, historical calibration reduces Brier error by 16.2%, with the improvement concentrated in success-level information. Search gains have a standard deviation of 21.4 percentage points, and tested historical estimators explain less than 2% of this variance in point estimates. Matched-configuration learning curves probe this distinction across training scales. At the reference token price, free selection observations yield a 2.49-point utility opportunity over the best constant action, driven primarily by gain information. Reusable histories, frozen splits, strong baselines and scoring tools provide a common foundation for developing and evaluating value-aware search agents.

Abstract HaMo combines rapid retrieval of procedural experience cards with gradual model adaptation to improve long-horizon web agents' retention and cross-task execution.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Long-horizon agents continuously encounter reusable procedural knowledge, yet existing systems typically rely either on model adaptation or external memory, trading persistent learning for rapid reuse. This limitation is especially important for Web agents that repeatedly execute related multi-step workflows. We propose HaMo, a Harness-Model collaborative framework that combines fast non-parametric experience accumulation with slow parametric adaptation. HaMo distills trajectories into strategy-level experience cards for immediate retrieval, while an experience-conditioned continual learning objective trains the model to interpret, ground, and execute retrieved strategies across changing contexts. A retrieval-coverage-based selection mechanism further identifies compact and complementary trajectories for parameter updates. Experiments show that HaMo outperforms harness-only and model-only adaptation under within-domain and cross-domain shifts, improving both retention and adaptation. These results highlight the importance of jointly preserving procedural experience and internalizing the competence to transfer and execute it across tasks.

Траектории выполнения задач сжимаются в карточки опыта, которые извлекаются из внешней памяти и подаются модели, а continual learning-цель обучает модель интерпретировать и выполнять эти стратегии в меняющихся контекстах. Механизм отбора на основе покрытия при извлечении выбирает компактные и взаимодополняющие траектории для обновления параметров.
